Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and other nights I stand in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s gone to bed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has just been... not normal for me lately. It’s kind of all over the place. Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I’ll make it or sit down with it and then just push it around, and other nights it’s the complete opposite. I’ll be standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, just sort of picking at things and then realizing I’ve been doing it for a while.

It’s embarrassing to say because it sounds small, maybe, but it’s been noticeable enough that I thought, okay, something is off and I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Usually before it happens, it’s just been one of those days where my eating is already off. Like, sometimes I barely eat dinner, or I sit there with the plate and I’m not really interested in it, and then later at night, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting a drink or maybe one little thing, and then it turns into crackers or something sweet, just picking at things while I’m standing there.

Afterward I mostly feel kind of embarrassed and annoyed with myself, because it feels like I didn’t even make a real decision to eat. It’s not like a planned meal. It’s more like I suddenly notice, oh, I’ve been standing here eating for a while.

As far as compensating… I don’t really have some set thing I do afterward. The bigger pattern is just that my appetite feels all over the place—some weeks barely touching dinner, and other nights doing that kitchen snacking thing after everyone’s asleep. That’s what made me feel like something is off.

It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.

Like, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.

So I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.

That’s what made me feel like something is off.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night like that, does it feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating, or does it feel more like you're just nibbling without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.

I think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. It’s not like I’m out during the day and suddenly doing that same grazing thing in the middle of everything. During the day it’s more that I might not be hungry or I don’t finish food. Then later at night it kind of swings the other way, and I’m picking at stuff without really meaning to.

That’s what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep.

That’s what made me feel like something is off.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night like that, does it feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating, or does it feel more like you're just nibbling without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually only find yourself eating like this at night because you're awake, or does it also happen during the day when you're up and about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. It’s not like I’m out during the day and suddenly doing that same grazing thing in the middle of everything. During the day it’s more that I might not be hungry or I don’t finish food. Then later at night it kind of swings the other way, and I’m picking at stuff without really meaning to.\n\nThat’s what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, I do think that happens, at least some of the time. During the day I can kind of go through the motions and not really feel hungry, or I’ll know it’s dinnertime and I should eat something, but I just don’t have much interest in it. It’s not like I’m making a decision to skip meals in some organized way, it’s more like food just doesn’t appeal to me, or I put it off and then suddenly realize I barely ate.

Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights it flips around and I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or something sweet. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has just been... not normal for me lately. It’s kind of all over the place. Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I’ll make it or sit down with it and then just push it around, and other nights it’s the complete opposite. I’ll be standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, just sort of picking at things and then realizing I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say because it sounds small, maybe, but it’s been noticeable enough that I thought, okay, something is off and I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what typically happens before and after these nighttime eating episodes? Do you ever try to compensate for them, like by skipping meals, exercising more, or making yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: Usually before it happens, it’s just been one of those days where my eating is already off. Like, sometimes I barely eat dinner, or I sit there with the plate and I’m not really interested in it, and then later at night, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting a drink or maybe one little thing, and then it turns into crackers or something sweet, just picking at things while I’m standing there.\n\nAfterward I mostly feel kind of embarrassed and annoyed with myself, because it feels like I didn’t even make a real decision to eat. It’s not like a planned meal. It’s more like I suddenly notice, oh, I’ve been standing here eating for a while.\n\nAs far as compensating… I don’t really have some set thing I do afterward. The bigger pattern is just that my appetite feels all over the place—some weeks barely touching dinner, and other nights doing that kitchen snacking thing after everyone’s asleep. That’s what made me feel like something is off.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night like that, does it feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating, or does it feel more like you're just nibbling without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually only find yourself eating like this at night because you're awake, or does it also happen during the day when you're up and about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. It’s not like I’m out during the day and suddenly doing that same grazing thing in the middle of everything. During the day it’s more that I might not be hungry or I don’t finish food. Then later at night it kind of swings the other way, and I’m picking at stuff without really meaning to.\n\nThat’s what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not feeling hungry during the day, or not having any interest in eating even when you know you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think that happens, at least some of the time. During the day I can kind of go through the motions and not really feel hungry, or I’ll know it’s dinnertime and I should eat something, but I just don’t have much interest in it. It’s not like I’m making a decision to skip meals in some organized way, it’s more like food just doesn’t appeal to me, or I put it off and then suddenly realize I barely ate.\n\nAnd then that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it doesn’t stay that way consistently. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights it flips around and I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or something sweet. Not a real meal, just picking at things. That back-and-forth is really what finally made me think, okay, this isn’t just a normal appetite thing for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s kind of both, but I don’t think it’s always true physical hunger. Sometimes I’ll realize, well, I barely ate dinner or I didn’t really eat much earlier, so maybe part of it is my body catching up. But other times I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m starving.” It’s more like I’m awake, the house is quiet, and then I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet almost before I’ve really decided to.

That back-and-forth is really what finally made me think, okay, this isn’t just a normal appetite thing for me.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night after everyone’s gone to bed, is it usually because you feel physically hungry, or do you sometimes eat even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I don’t think it’s always true physical hunger. Sometimes I’ll realize, well, I barely ate dinner or I didn’t really eat much earlier, so maybe part of it is my body catching up. But other times I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m starving.” It’s more like I’m awake, the house is quiet, and then I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet almost before I’ve really decided to.\n\nThat’s what’s been bothering me, I guess—the inconsistency. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m grazing on stuff like crackers and sweets. It doesn’t feel like a normal meal or a planned snack. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place, and that’s honestly what finally pushed me to come in.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you find yourself eating in the kitchen at night after everyone’s gone to bed, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're in a kind of daze or not completely present?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think I’m awake, mostly. It doesn’t feel like I’m sleepwalking or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening things and eating them. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.

So I’m aware, but not very intentional. And that’s part of what’s been bothering me, because it’s not just one clear pattern. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like I’ll just have no interest and leave most of it, and then other nights it’s the opposite where I’m in the kitchen late eating little things after everyone has gone to bed. It’s not like a normal meal. It’s more this grazing thing, and it feels kind of scattered.

That’s really what pushed me to come in, honestly. It just started feeling like my eating was all over the place, and I couldn’t tell if I should be worried or if I was just being dramatic about it.

I’m not sure I could give you an exact number, honestly. It’s not every single night, but it’s enough that I started noticing it as a pattern. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.

Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I’ll make it or sit down with it and then just push it around, and other nights it’s the complete opposite. I’ll be standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, just sort of picking at things and then realizing I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say because it sounds small, maybe, but it’s been noticeable enough that I thought, okay, something is off and I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what typically happens before and after these nighttime eating episodes? Do you ever try to compensate for them, like by skipping meals, exercising more, or making yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: Usually before it happens, it’s just been one of those days where my eating is already off. Like, sometimes I barely eat dinner, or I sit there with the plate and I’m not really interested in it, and then later at night, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting a drink or maybe one little thing, and then it turns into crackers or something sweet, just picking at things while I’m standing there.\n\nAfterward I mostly feel kind of embarrassed and annoyed with myself, because it feels like I didn’t even make a real decision to eat. It’s not like a planned meal. It’s more like I suddenly notice, oh, I’ve been standing here eating for a while.\n\nAs far as compensating… I don’t really have some set thing I do afterward. The bigger pattern is just that my appetite feels all over the place—some weeks barely touching dinner, and other nights doing that kitchen snacking thing after everyone’s asleep. That’s what made me feel like something is off.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night like that, does it feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating, or does it feel more like you're just nibbling without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually only find yourself eating like this at night because you're awake, or does it also happen during the day when you're up and about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. I’ll be standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, just sort of picking at things and then realizing I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say because it sounds small, maybe, but it’s been noticeable enough that I thought, okay, something is off and I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what typically happens before and after these nighttime eating episodes? Do you ever try to compensate for them, like by skipping meals, exercising more, or making yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: Usually before it happens, it’s just been one of those days where my eating is already off. Like, sometimes I barely eat dinner, or I sit there with the plate and I’m not really interested in it, and then later at night, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting a drink or maybe one little thing, and then it turns into crackers or something sweet, just picking at things while I’m standing there.\n\nAfterward I mostly feel kind of embarrassed and annoyed with myself, because it feels like I didn’t even make a real decision to eat. It’s not like a planned meal. It’s more like I suddenly notice, oh, I’ve been standing here eating for a while.\n\nAs far as compensating… I don’t really have some set thing I do afterward. The bigger pattern is just that my appetite feels all over the place—some weeks barely touching dinner, and other nights doing that kitchen snacking thing after everyone’s asleep. That’s what made me feel like something is off.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night like that, does it feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating, or does it feel more like you're just nibbling without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually only find yourself eating like this at night because you're awake, or does it also happen during the day when you're up and about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. It’s not like I’m out during the day and suddenly doing that same grazing thing in the middle of everything. During the day it’s more that I might not be hungry or I don’t finish food. Then later at night it kind of swings the other way, and I’m picking at stuff without really meaning to.\n\nThat’s what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not feeling hungry during the day, or not having any interest in eating even when you know you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think that happens, at least some of the time. During the day I can kind of go through the motions and not really feel hungry, or I’ll know it’s dinnertime and I should eat something, but I just don’t have much interest in it. It’s not like I’m making a decision to skip meals in some organized way, it’s more like food just doesn’t appeal to me, or I put it off and then suddenly realize I barely ate.\n\nAnd then that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it doesn’t stay that way consistently. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights it flips around and I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or something sweet. Not a real meal, just picking at things. That back-and-forth is really what finally made me think, okay, this isn’t just a normal appetite thing for me.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night after everyone’s gone to bed, is it usually because you feel physically hungry, or do you sometimes eat even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I don’t think it’s always true physical hunger. Sometimes I’ll realize, well, I barely ate dinner or I didn’t really eat much earlier, so maybe part of it is my body catching up. But other times I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m starving.” It’s more like I’m awake, the house is quiet, and then I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet almost before I’ve really decided to.\n\nThat’s what’s been bothering me, I guess—the inconsistency. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m grazing on stuff like crackers and sweets. It doesn’t feel like a normal meal or a planned snack. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place, and that’s honestly what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating in the kitchen at night after everyone’s gone to bed, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're in a kind of daze or not completely present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m awake, mostly. It doesn’t feel like I’m sleepwalking or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening things and eating them. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.\n\nSo I’m aware, but not very intentional. And that’s part of what’s been bothering me, because it’s not just one clear pattern. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like I’ll just have no interest and leave most of it, and then other nights it’s the opposite where I’m in the kitchen late eating little things after everyone has gone to bed. It’s not like a normal meal. It’s more this grazing thing, and it feels kind of scattered.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in, honestly. It just started feeling like my eating was all over the place, and I couldn’t tell if I should be worried or if I was just being dramatic about it.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these nighttime eating episodes happen—like, how many nights a week or month do you find yourself eating like this after everyone has gone to bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could give you an exact number, honestly. It’s not every single night, but it’s enough that I started noticing it as a pattern. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.\n\nIt’s more that my eating feels really inconsistent. Like, I’ll have days where dinner just doesn’t appeal to me and I leave most of it, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. Not sitting down for a meal, just kind of grazing. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, because I couldn’t tell what was going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you have little to no appetite during the day, even when it's time for meals like lunch or dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s part of what I’m trying to explain, I guess. During the day it’s not always that I feel sick or anything, but meals just don’t appeal to me sometimes. Like dinner especially—I’ll sit there with everyone and kind of pick at it, and then realize I barely ate any of it. Some weeks it feels like I’m barely touching dinner at all.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay consistent, which is what’s confusing me. Because other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not like a real meal, just sort of grazing. So it’s not simply “I never have an appetite,” but my eating has been all over the place enough that it started worrying me. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.\n\nSo I’m aware, but not very intentional. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.\n\nIt’s more that my eating feels really inconsistent. Like, I’ll have days where dinner just doesn’t appeal to me and I leave most of it, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. Not sitting down for a meal, just kind of grazing. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, because I couldn’t tell what was going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you have little to no appetite during the day, even when it's time for meals like lunch or dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s part of what I’m trying to explain, I guess. Because other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not like a real meal, just sort of grazing. So it’s not simply “I never have an appetite,” but my eating has been all over the place enough that it started worrying me. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy most days, even if just a little bit?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute of every day, but I do go through these stretches where everything in the house just feels kind of gray and heavy. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing especially terrible has happened that day, nobody said anything awful, there isn’t some big crisis, but I’ll just feel… empty.

It’s hard to explain because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just sitting there, maybe staring off or being quiet, but inside it feels like the air has gotten heavier somehow. Things I normally might enjoy or at least feel interested in don’t really pull me in the same way during those times. I’m there, but I’m not really feeling much connection to anything.

And then I start questioning myself, like, “Why are you feeling this way? Nothing even happened.” But that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s just this flat, gray feeling that settles over me for a while.

Like, sometimes I barely eat dinner, or I sit there with the plate and I’m not really interested in it, and then later at night, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting a drink or maybe one little thing, and then it turns into crackers or something sweet, just picking at things while I’m standing there.\n\nAfterward I mostly feel kind of embarrassed and annoyed with myself, because it feels like I didn’t even make a real decision to eat. It’s not like a planned meal. It’s more like I suddenly notice, oh, I’ve been standing here eating for a while.\n\nAs far as compensating… I don’t really have some set thing I do afterward. The bigger pattern is just that my appetite feels all over the place—some weeks barely touching dinner, and other nights doing that kitchen snacking thing after everyone’s asleep. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually only find yourself eating like this at night because you're awake, or does it also happen during the day when you're up and about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. During the day I can kind of go through the motions and not really feel hungry, or I’ll know it’s dinnertime and I should eat something, but I just don’t have much interest in it. It’s not like I’m making a decision to skip meals in some organized way, it’s more like food just doesn’t appeal to me, or I put it off and then suddenly realize I barely ate.\n\nAnd then that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it doesn’t stay that way consistently. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights it flips around and I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or something sweet. Not a real meal, just picking at things. That back-and-forth is really what finally made me think, okay, this isn’t just a normal appetite thing for me.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night after everyone’s gone to bed, is it usually because you feel physically hungry, or do you sometimes eat even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I don’t think it’s always true physical hunger. Sometimes I’ll realize, well, I barely ate dinner or I didn’t really eat much earlier, so maybe part of it is my body catching up. But other times I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m starving.” It’s more like I’m awake, the house is quiet, and then I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet almost before I’ve really decided to.\n\nThat’s what’s been bothering me, I guess—the inconsistency. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m grazing on stuff like crackers and sweets. It doesn’t feel like a normal meal or a planned snack. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place, and that’s honestly what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating in the kitchen at night after everyone’s gone to bed, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're in a kind of daze or not completely present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m awake, mostly. It doesn’t feel like I’m sleepwalking or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening things and eating them. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.\n\nSo I’m aware, but not very intentional. And that’s part of what’s been bothering me, because it’s not just one clear pattern. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like I’ll just have no interest and leave most of it, and then other nights it’s the opposite where I’m in the kitchen late eating little things after everyone has gone to bed. It’s not like a normal meal. It’s more this grazing thing, and it feels kind of scattered.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in, honestly. It just started feeling like my eating was all over the place, and I couldn’t tell if I should be worried or if I was just being dramatic about it.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these nighttime eating episodes happen—like, how many nights a week or month do you find yourself eating like this after everyone has gone to bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could give you an exact number, honestly. It’s not every single night, but it’s enough that I started noticing it as a pattern. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.\n\nIt’s more that my eating feels really inconsistent. Like, I’ll have days where dinner just doesn’t appeal to me and I leave most of it, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. Not sitting down for a meal, just kind of grazing. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, because I couldn’t tell what was going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you have little to no appetite during the day, even when it's time for meals like lunch or dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s part of what I’m trying to explain, I guess. During the day it’s not always that I feel sick or anything, but meals just don’t appeal to me sometimes. Like dinner especially—I’ll sit there with everyone and kind of pick at it, and then realize I barely ate any of it. Some weeks it feels like I’m barely touching dinner at all.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay consistent, which is what’s confusing me. Because other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not like a real meal, just sort of grazing. So it’s not simply “I never have an appetite,” but my eating has been all over the place enough that it started worrying me. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy most days, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute of every day, but I do go through these stretches where everything in the house just feels kind of gray and heavy. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing especially terrible has happened that day, nobody said anything awful, there isn’t some big crisis, but I’ll just feel… empty.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just sitting there, maybe staring off or being quiet, but inside it feels like the air has gotten heavier somehow. Things I normally might enjoy or at least feel interested in don’t really pull me in the same way during those times. I’m there, but I’m not really feeling much connection to anything.\n\nAnd then I start questioning myself, like, “Why are you feeling this way? Nothing even happened.” But that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s just this flat, gray feeling that settles over me for a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… I’d say most days during those stretches, even if it’s not always dramatic. It’s more like this heaviness settles in. The house feels gray, kind of heavy, and I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table just feeling empty, even though nothing terrible happened that day.

Then later at night it kind of swings the other way, and I’m picking at stuff without really meaning to.\n\nThat’s what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not feeling hungry during the day, or not having any interest in eating even when you know you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think that happens, at least some of the time. During the day I can kind of go through the motions and not really feel hungry, or I’ll know it’s dinnertime and I should eat something, but I just don’t have much interest in it. It’s not like I’m making a decision to skip meals in some organized way, it’s more like food just doesn’t appeal to me, or I put it off and then suddenly realize I barely ate.\n\nAnd then that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it doesn’t stay that way consistently. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights it flips around and I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or something sweet. Not a real meal, just picking at things. That back-and-forth is really what finally made me think, okay, this isn’t just a normal appetite thing for me.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night after everyone’s gone to bed, is it usually because you feel physically hungry, or do you sometimes eat even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I don’t think it’s always true physical hunger. Sometimes I’ll realize, well, I barely ate dinner or I didn’t really eat much earlier, so maybe part of it is my body catching up. But other times I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m starving.” It’s more like I’m awake, the house is quiet, and then I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet almost before I’ve really decided to.\n\nThat’s what’s been bothering me, I guess—the inconsistency. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m grazing on stuff like crackers and sweets. It doesn’t feel like a normal meal or a planned snack. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place, and that’s honestly what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating in the kitchen at night after everyone’s gone to bed, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're in a kind of daze or not completely present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m awake, mostly. It doesn’t feel like I’m sleepwalking or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening things and eating them. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.\n\nSo I’m aware, but not very intentional. And that’s part of what’s been bothering me, because it’s not just one clear pattern. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like I’ll just have no interest and leave most of it, and then other nights it’s the opposite where I’m in the kitchen late eating little things after everyone has gone to bed. It’s not like a normal meal. It’s more this grazing thing, and it feels kind of scattered.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in, honestly. It just started feeling like my eating was all over the place, and I couldn’t tell if I should be worried or if I was just being dramatic about it.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these nighttime eating episodes happen—like, how many nights a week or month do you find yourself eating like this after everyone has gone to bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could give you an exact number, honestly. It’s not every single night, but it’s enough that I started noticing it as a pattern. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.\n\nIt’s more that my eating feels really inconsistent. Like, I’ll have days where dinner just doesn’t appeal to me and I leave most of it, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. Not sitting down for a meal, just kind of grazing. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, because I couldn’t tell what was going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you have little to no appetite during the day, even when it's time for meals like lunch or dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s part of what I’m trying to explain, I guess. During the day it’s not always that I feel sick or anything, but meals just don’t appeal to me sometimes. Like dinner especially—I’ll sit there with everyone and kind of pick at it, and then realize I barely ate any of it. Some weeks it feels like I’m barely touching dinner at all.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay consistent, which is what’s confusing me. Because other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not like a real meal, just sort of grazing. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy most days, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute of every day, but I do go through these stretches where everything in the house just feels kind of gray and heavy. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing especially terrible has happened that day, nobody said anything awful, there isn’t some big crisis, but I’ll just feel… empty.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just sitting there, maybe staring off or being quiet, but inside it feels like the air has gotten heavier somehow. Things I normally might enjoy or at least feel interested in don’t really pull me in the same way during those times. I’m there, but I’m not really feeling much connection to anything.\n\nAnd then I start questioning myself, like, “Why are you feeling this way? Nothing even happened.” But that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s just this flat, gray feeling that settles over me for a while.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down, depressed, or have little interest in things most days, even if it's just a mild feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say most days during those stretches, even if it’s not always dramatic. It’s more like this heaviness settles in. The house feels gray, kind of heavy, and I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table just feeling empty, even though nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because I can’t always point to a reason. It’s not like there was a fight or some big bad news. I’m just sitting there, and everything feels flat and kind of far away. It’s more like this heaviness settles in. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t really tracked the number closely, so I don’t want to say, like, “I lost exactly this much,” because I honestly don’t know. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly.

But what I have noticed is that my eating has been really all over the place, and that’s what finally pushed me to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit down like I’m going to eat with everyone, and then I just kind of move the food around or take a few bites and feel done. And then other nights, it’s the opposite in this weird way, where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll end up standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not really hungry exactly, just… grazing.

So I don’t know if my weight has changed significantly, but the pattern feels off enough that I’ve been worried about it.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has just been... not normal for me lately. It’s kind of all over the place. Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I’ll make it or sit down with it and then just push it around, and other nights it’s the complete opposite. I’ll be standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, just sort of picking at things and then realizing I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say because it sounds small, maybe, but it’s been noticeable enough that I thought, okay, something is off and I should probably talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what typically happens before and after these nighttime eating episodes? Do you ever try to compensate for them, like by skipping meals, exercising more, or making yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: Usually before it happens, it’s just been one of those days where my eating is already off. Like, sometimes I barely eat dinner, or I sit there with the plate and I’m not really interested in it, and then later at night, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting a drink or maybe one little thing, and then it turns into crackers or something sweet, just picking at things while I’m standing there.\n\nAfterward I mostly feel kind of embarrassed and annoyed with myself, because it feels like I didn’t even make a real decision to eat. It’s not like a planned meal. It’s more like I suddenly notice, oh, I’ve been standing here eating for a while.\n\nAs far as compensating… I don’t really have some set thing I do afterward. The bigger pattern is just that my appetite feels all over the place—some weeks barely touching dinner, and other nights doing that kitchen snacking thing after everyone’s asleep. That’s what made me feel like something is off.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night like that, does it feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating, or does it feel more like you're just nibbling without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like nibbling without really thinking about it, I think. I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely out of control, like I’m panicking or shoveling food in or anything like that. It’s more that I’ll be in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, and I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and then I sort of lose track of it. Not in a dramatic way, just… I realize I’ve been standing there eating longer than I meant to.\n\nThat’s part of why it bothered me enough to come in, because my eating has been so inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m doing that late-night kitchen thing, and it doesn’t feel like a normal appetite pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after a stressful or emotionally difficult day, or do they seem to happen regardless of how your day went emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I keep trying to look back and figure out if it’s always after a bad day, and I can’t say there’s a clean pattern like that. What I’ve noticed more is just that my appetite itself has been all over the place.\n\nLike, some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, even if I made it or I know I should eat, and then other nights I’m suddenly in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and sort of grazing. It’s not always that I can point to one stressful thing and say, “That’s why I did it.” Sometimes it just seems to happen because I didn’t really eat normally earlier, or because I’m up and the house is quiet, and then I’m picking at things.\n\nSo I guess emotionally difficult days might make it easier to happen, maybe, but I don’t know that it only happens then. The main thing that pushed me to come in is just how inconsistent it’s gotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually only find yourself eating like this at night because you're awake, or does it also happen during the day when you're up and about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at night, from what I’ve noticed. I mean, during the day my eating can still be strange in the sense that sometimes I just don’t really eat much, or I’ll sort of ignore meals, but the standing-in-the-kitchen thing with crackers or something sweet is usually after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI think the quiet of the house has something to do with it, or just the fact that I’m still awake and there’s no real structure then. It’s not like I’m out during the day and suddenly doing that same grazing thing in the middle of everything. During the day it’s more that I might not be hungry or I don’t finish food. Then later at night it kind of swings the other way, and I’m picking at stuff without really meaning to.\n\nThat’s what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not feeling hungry during the day, or not having any interest in eating even when you know you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think that happens, at least some of the time. During the day I can kind of go through the motions and not really feel hungry, or I’ll know it’s dinnertime and I should eat something, but I just don’t have much interest in it. It’s not like I’m making a decision to skip meals in some organized way, it’s more like food just doesn’t appeal to me, or I put it off and then suddenly realize I barely ate.\n\nAnd then that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it doesn’t stay that way consistently. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights it flips around and I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or something sweet. Not a real meal, just picking at things. That back-and-forth is really what finally made me think, okay, this isn’t just a normal appetite thing for me.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating at night after everyone’s gone to bed, is it usually because you feel physically hungry, or do you sometimes eat even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I don’t think it’s always true physical hunger. Sometimes I’ll realize, well, I barely ate dinner or I didn’t really eat much earlier, so maybe part of it is my body catching up. But other times I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m starving.” It’s more like I’m awake, the house is quiet, and then I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet almost before I’ve really decided to.\n\nThat’s what’s been bothering me, I guess—the inconsistency. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m grazing on stuff like crackers and sweets. It doesn’t feel like a normal meal or a planned snack. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place, and that’s honestly what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating in the kitchen at night after everyone’s gone to bed, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're in a kind of daze or not completely present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m awake, mostly. It doesn’t feel like I’m sleepwalking or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening things and eating them. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.\n\nSo I’m aware, but not very intentional. And that’s part of what’s been bothering me, because it’s not just one clear pattern. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like I’ll just have no interest and leave most of it, and then other nights it’s the opposite where I’m in the kitchen late eating little things after everyone has gone to bed. It’s not like a normal meal. It’s more this grazing thing, and it feels kind of scattered.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in, honestly. It just started feeling like my eating was all over the place, and I couldn’t tell if I should be worried or if I was just being dramatic about it.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these nighttime eating episodes happen—like, how many nights a week or month do you find yourself eating like this after everyone has gone to bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could give you an exact number, honestly. It’s not every single night, but it’s enough that I started noticing it as a pattern. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.\n\nIt’s more that my eating feels really inconsistent. Like, I’ll have days where dinner just doesn’t appeal to me and I leave most of it, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. Not sitting down for a meal, just kind of grazing. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, because I couldn’t tell what was going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you have little to no appetite during the day, even when it's time for meals like lunch or dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s part of what I’m trying to explain, I guess. During the day it’s not always that I feel sick or anything, but meals just don’t appeal to me sometimes. Like dinner especially—I’ll sit there with everyone and kind of pick at it, and then realize I barely ate any of it. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place, and that’s honestly what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating in the kitchen at night after everyone’s gone to bed, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're in a kind of daze or not completely present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m awake, mostly. It doesn’t feel like I’m sleepwalking or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know I’m opening things and eating them. But it can feel sort of automatic, if that makes sense—like I’m not sitting down and deciding, “I’m going to have a snack now.” It’s more like I’m up, the house is quiet, everyone’s asleep, and then I realize I’m standing there with crackers or something sweet.\n\nSo I’m aware, but not very intentional. And that’s part of what’s been bothering me, because it’s not just one clear pattern. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like I’ll just have no interest and leave most of it, and then other nights it’s the opposite where I’m in the kitchen late eating little things after everyone has gone to bed. It’s not like a normal meal. It’s more this grazing thing, and it feels kind of scattered.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in, honestly. It just started feeling like my eating was all over the place, and I couldn’t tell if I should be worried or if I was just being dramatic about it.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these nighttime eating episodes happen—like, how many nights a week or month do you find yourself eating like this after everyone has gone to bed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could give you an exact number, honestly. It’s not every single night, but it’s enough that I started noticing it as a pattern. Some weeks it might happen a few nights, especially if I’ve barely eaten dinner or just picked at it, and then other weeks it’s less obvious.\n\nIt’s more that my eating feels really inconsistent. Like, I’ll have days where dinner just doesn’t appeal to me and I leave most of it, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. Not sitting down for a meal, just kind of grazing. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, because I couldn’t tell what was going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you have little to no appetite during the day, even when it's time for meals like lunch or dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s part of what I’m trying to explain, I guess. During the day it’s not always that I feel sick or anything, but meals just don’t appeal to me sometimes. Like dinner especially—I’ll sit there with everyone and kind of pick at it, and then realize I barely ate any of it. Some weeks it feels like I’m barely touching dinner at all.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay consistent, which is what’s confusing me. Because other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not like a real meal, just sort of grazing. So it’s not simply “I never have an appetite,” but my eating has been all over the place enough that it started worrying me. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy most days, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single minute of every day, but I do go through these stretches where everything in the house just feels kind of gray and heavy. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing especially terrible has happened that day, nobody said anything awful, there isn’t some big crisis, but I’ll just feel… empty.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just sitting there, maybe staring off or being quiet, but inside it feels like the air has gotten heavier somehow. Things I normally might enjoy or at least feel interested in don’t really pull me in the same way during those times. I’m there, but I’m not really feeling much connection to anything.\n\nAnd then I start questioning myself, like, “Why are you feeling this way? Nothing even happened.” But that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s just this flat, gray feeling that settles over me for a while.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down, depressed, or have little interest in things most days, even if it's just a mild feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say most days during those stretches, even if it’s not always dramatic. It’s more like this heaviness settles in. The house feels gray, kind of heavy, and I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table just feeling empty, even though nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me, because I can’t always point to a reason. It’s not like there was a fight or some big bad news. I’m just sitting there, and everything feels flat and kind of far away. So yes, I think there is a depressed feeling there, even if sometimes it’s mild or I try to tell myself it shouldn’t count because I’m still up and moving around.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss or a significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really tracked the number closely, so I don’t want to say, like, “I lost exactly this much,” because I honestly don’t know. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly.\n\nBut what I have noticed is that my eating has been really all over the place, and that’s what finally pushed me to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit down like I’m going to eat with everyone, and then I just kind of move the food around or take a few bites and feel done. And then other nights, it’s the opposite in this weird way, where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll end up standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet, not really hungry exactly, just… grazing.\n\nSo I don’t know if my weight has changed significantly, but the pattern feels off enough that I’ve been worried about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel unlike myself.

There are things I used to look forward to, not even huge things, just normal parts of my life. Like book club — I used to enjoy reading something and having an opinion about it, even if I didn’t love the book. I liked hearing what everyone else thought. Lately it can feel like another appointment I’m supposed to drag myself to, or I’ll put off reading and then feel guilty about it.

Cooking is like that too. I used to like planning a meal or trying something a little different, and now sometimes it just feels like standing in front of the counter doing a list of tasks. Even going to galleries, which used to feel kind of refreshing for me, can feel like too much effort, like I’m just walking through because I’m supposed to enjoy it.

And the rose beds… that one bothers me. I used to really care about them, checking on them, trimming things back, noticing new growth. Now I’ll see they need attention and I just keep putting it off. It starts to feel less like something I love and more like a chore sitting there waiting for me.
